Probabilistic Regular Tree Priors for Scientific Symbolic Reasoning
Tim Schneider, Amin Totounferoush, Wolfgang Nowak, Steffen Staab

Abstract
Symbolic Regression (SR) allows for the discovery of scientific equations from data. To limit the large search space of possible equations, prior knowledge has been expressed in terms of formal grammars that characterize subsets of arbitrary strings. However, it is very challenging to formalize the aggregation of two or more items of prior knowledge, because context-free grammars are not closed under intersection. To address this issue, we propose to (i) compactly express experts’ beliefs about which equations are more likely to be expected by Probabilistic Regular Tree Expressions (pRTEs) , and (ii) efficiently encode such priors as finite state machines during Bayesian inference for symbolic regression. Our experiments show its effectiveness in scientific applications including soil and material science.



1
Introduction

A significant aspect of the scientific process in natural science is to formulate new research hypotheses based on experimental data
[
32
]
.

Artificial Intelligence (AI)
has been proposed as a
new tool

[
39
]
for scientists to gain understanding
[
20
]
and support hypotheses formulation.
Bayesian methods
[
16
;
11
]
for the long-established task of symbolic regression
[
6
]
have become key candidates to aid scientists, because of their well-defined posterior of symbolic solutions given priors and evidence in the form of experimental data.

Centuries of research in natural sciences have yielded a vast amount of knowledge.
Bayesian frameworks can encode such knowledge as prior distributions
and thus exploit this knowledge to guide the search for symbolic expressions.
Standard approaches to encoding prior knowledge about the structure of the unknown equation would leverage formal languages over
strings
.
These approaches exhibit several weaknesses:

(i)

Sets of syntactically correct arithmetic expressions cannot be described by
regular
languages. The simple language over strings matching the numbers and structures of opening and closing parenthesis, known as
Dyck’s language
, is not regular. This renders regular string languages not expressive enough for many languages of practical interest. Thus typically
Probabilistic Context-Free Grammar (PCFG)
are used. But

(ii)

context-free
grammars are not closed under boolean operations
[
15
]
, making arbitrary combinations of prior knowledge from different sources infeasible.

Syntactically correct arithmetic expressions are trees. For example,
\left((x-7)^{2}+(x+z)^{2}\right)+12
is a tree with
+
as the root node,
12
as its child to the right and a continuing subtree to the left.

Bayesian
Inference
(

4.2
)
Data
Experts’
Prior
Knowledge
(
Section

5
)
pRTE

Posterior

Figure 1
:
Scientists typically have data and prior knowledge. Our
Bayesian Inference
(
Section

4.2
) requires
samples
(
Section

4.3
) and
density evaluations
(
Section

4.4
) of the latter and yields a posterior distribution over expressions that fit the data consistently to the prior knowledge.

We propose to deal with these drawbacks of
string grammars
by encoding priors using formal
tree languages

[
5
]
and suggest a Bayesian inference method for symbolic regression with it. Formal tree grammars can easily be used to
generate trees
and from these trees generate strings, but formal tree grammars cannot be used directly for parsing strings, only for parsing trees. For the previous equation, this means that we can not check whether it is syntactically correct, but given it is we can use a finite state machine that assigns it a probability under a prior.
With these ideas, it is possible to define
expressive
regular languages with:

(i)

compact
Probabilistic Regular Tree Expression (pRTE)

[
37
]
, and

(ii)

closure properties
[
30
]
under Boolean operations.

Overview

For discovering the law of gravitational force a scientist might know that it must be translational invariant.
As depicted in
Figure

1
, she expresses this
prior knowledge
through a
pRTE
.
Knowing additionally that the physical units in a force function must match and expressing this through another
pRTE
,
the closure properties
allow us to automatically translate it into a joint
Probabilistic Tree Automaton (PTA)
.
Our inference algorithm (
Section

4.2
) works on the data interacting with the
pRTE
to generate proposals (
Section

4.3
), as well as with the
PTA
to evaluate densities (
Section

4.4
). It yields a
posterior distribution
over arithmetic expressions fitting the data evidence and respecting the experts’ prior knowledge.

2
Background

Symbolic Regression (SR)
identifies a
symbolic expression
, i.e. an analytical equation
f
, that explains a given set of data
\mathcal{D}=\left\{(x_{i},y_{i})\right\}_{i=0}^{n}
, i.e.
\forall x,y\in\mathcal{D}:f(x)\approx y
.
There are infinitely many possible solutions
f
that fit the data up to an error bound, but many of them constitute
physically unreasonable
solutions and thus further preferences may be expressed by
formal languages
.

2.1
Formal String Languages

A
formal language
is any subset
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
of the set of strings
\Sigma^{*}
that can be composed out of a finite set of symbols
\Sigma
, called an alphabet.
All finite words
w\in L
contained in a language can be generated with a set of rules collected in a
grammar
, while the membership of
\tilde{w}\in\Sigma^{*}
in
L
can be determined through a
automaton
.
Different classes of grammars and automata form a hierarchy of increasing computational complexity of languages, known as
Chomsky Hierarchy

[
4
]
.
The simplest class comprises
regular languages
.
While those have useful properties, e.g. they are efficiently parseable and are closed under Boolean operations, they are not very expressive. Even correct nesting of parenthesis, which is core to arithmetic formulas, cannot be described at an arbitrary depth.

Context-Free Grammars (CFGs)
allow for expressing arithmetic formulas at arbitrary depth, but their disadvantage is that they are not closed under intersection.
Both regular and context-free grammars can be described by production rules that also include probabilities, e.g.
Probabilistic Context-Free Grammar (PCFG)
, and thus express which words belong to a language
L
and how probable it is that a word
w
is produced by
L
, i.e.
P(w|L)
.
Let
x,y\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
be strings of natural numbers, then
x_{\smile}y
is their concatenation.

2.2
Formal Tree Languages & Series

Syntactically correct mathematical expressions are trees.
To characterize sets of trees, one extends the above notion to a
ranked alphabet

\Sigma=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma^{(k)}
giving every symbol
f\in\Sigma^{(k)}
a
\operatorname{rank}(f)=k
and thus a defined number of descendants, e.g.
f(\cdot,\cdot)\in\Sigma^{(2)}
and
\operatorname{rank}(f)=2
. The set of all possible trees over
\Sigma
is
T_{\Sigma}
and every subset
L\subseteq T_{\Sigma}
is called a
formal tree language

[
5
]
.

Definition 2.1

(Tree)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a ranked alphabet, and
D\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
A map
t:D\rightarrow\Sigma
assigning each node address a symbol in
\Sigma
is a
tree
,
iff it is

(i)

finite,
0<|D|<\infty
,

(ii)

prefix-closed,
u_{\smile}v\in D\implies u\in D
, and

(iii)

\forall x\in D,f=t(x):\{j|x_{\smile}j\in D\}=\{0,\ldots,\operatorname{rank}(f)\}\subset\mathbb{N}
.

Language membership can be equivalently described by a characteristic function
p:T_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
, called
tree series
, with
p(t)=1\iff t\in L
.
With the extension
p:T_{\Sigma}\rightarrow[0,1]
, the membership is probabilistic and defines a probability distribution over
T_{\Sigma}
.
One defines different classes of tree languages.
Regular tree languages
are closed under Boolean operations:
Let
L_{1},L_{2}\subseteq T_{\Sigma}
be regular, then the union
L_{1}\cup L_{2}
, the intersection
L_{1}\cap L_{2}
, the set difference
L_{1}\setminus L_{2}
, and the complement
T_{\Sigma}\setminus L_{1}
are also regular tree languages
[
30
]
.
A finite automaton can be constructed to evaluate whether a tree
w\in T_{\Sigma}
belongs to a tree language
L
or not.

2.2.1
Probabilistic Tree Automata

A (top-down)
Probabilistic Tree Automaton (PTA)

[
37
]
is a tuple
(Q,\delta,F,\mu)
composed of

(i)

a finite set of states
Q
,

(ii)

a set of final state and symbol pairs
F\subseteq Q\times\Sigma^{(0)}
,

(iii)

an initial distribution of states
\mu\in\Delta(Q)
, and

(iv)

a transition function

\displaystyle\delta=\bigcup_{n}\delta_{n}\;\;,\;\delta_{n}:\Sigma^{(n)}\times Q\rightarrow\Delta_{0}(Q^{n}),

(1)

with
\mathbf{q}\in Q^{n}
denoting a tuple of
n
states. With
\Delta(Q)
we denote the set of discrete probability distributions over the finite set
Q
. And
\Delta_{0}=\Delta\cup\mathbf{0}
is augmented by the improper distribution
\mathbf{0}
that assigns zero probability to all states.
Transitions of the automaton
A
are probabilistic and a successful
run

r:D\rightarrow Q
is assigning a state
q\in Q
to each node in the tree
t
such that the states at the leaf nodes are contained in the final set
F
.

Let
\operatorname{inner}(t),\operatorname{leaf}(t)
denote the set of inner and respectively leaf nodes of a tree
t
.
Then the evaluation of the automaton
A
, and thus the value of
p(t)
, is the expectation

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{r}\left[(r(x),t(x))\in F\;\;\forall\;x\in\operatorname{leaf(t)}\right]=

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\{r\\
|(r(x),t(x))\in F\\
\forall\;x\in\operatorname{leaf(t)}\}\end{subarray}}{\sum}\hskip-15.00002pt\mu(r(\epsilon))\hskip-10.00002pt\prod_{x\in\operatorname{inner}(t)}\hskip-10.00002pt\delta_{(t(x),r(x))}\big(r(x_{\smile}1),\ldots,r(x_{\smile}n)\big)

(2)

of runs being successful. Every regular tree series can be recognized by such an automaton
[
30
;
37
]
.

2.2.2
Probabilistic Regular Tree Expression

Probabilistic Regular Tree Expression (pRTE)

[
37
]
compactly assign probabilities to sets of trees.

Example 1

(
pRTE
)
.

All trees with at least one
g

+
, being elements of a ranked alphabet, we denote
f\in\Sigma
as
f
.

In
Example

1
, we denote the set of trees that contain at least one
g
, with three operators:

(i)

weighted choice
(“
+
") chooses one of the sub-expressions according to the assigned weights,

(ii)

concatenation
(“
\circ_{x}
") replaces each occurrence of the variable
x
with an expression from the right, and

(iii)

infinity iteration
(“
\infty x
") iteratively replaces all occurrences of
x
(and respectively
y
for
\infty y
).

That way,
E_{1}
assigns e.g.
g(g(a))
a probability of
\frac{1}{48}=\frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{4}
and
f(b,b)
a probability of
0
.
An extended
Example

4
that explains this process step by step is provided in
Appendix

C
.

3
Related Work

Symbolic Regression

has been approached from various perspectives:

(i)

Informed Search Strategies
ranging from classical specialized evolution strategies in genetic programming
[
19
;
6
]
to select the best expression among a population to guiding search
[
34
;
35
;
23
]
by
heuristics
inspired from science like symmetry and separability tests;

(ii)

Optimization Based
by first solving a continuous optimization like
LASSO
in a library of terms
[
3
]
or optimizing a neural network
[
31
]
and extracting a symbolic expression from it afterwards;

(iii)

Representation Learning
[
21
;
26
]
to find an embedding space for equations that eases the regression task for classical search,

(iv)

Deep Learning Approaches
[
28
;
36
;
17
;
14
]
train neural networks supervised or with a reinforcing objective to predict matching expressions, and

(v)

Bayesian Approaches
, as described next.

Bayesian Approaches

need to combine reasoning about the symbolic structure of an equation with reasoning about (continuous) parameters inside it.
Proposing changes to the tree structure of an equation with a Markov Chain
[
16
;
11
]

and deal with varying aomounts of the parameters among symbolic candidates by

(i)

reversible jumps
[
16
]
in a joint space of parameters and auxiliary variables, or

(ii)

selecting structure candidates based on the
Bayesian Information Criterion (BIC)

[
11
]
.

While the latter is only a first-order approximation to the true posterior
[
9
]
and cuts the search space at a predefined depth, the former hard-codes a prior that does not generalize well in benchmarks
[
22
]
.
Assigning probabilities to
dictionaries
that capture discrete and continuous choices taken in a
probabilistic program

[
8
]
has symbolic regression as a special case.
All these approaches lack a compact and easily exchangeable representation of scientific prior knowledge.

Scientific Prior Knowledge

has mostly been expressed as context-free grammars over strings.
This includes general or domain-specific assumptions about equations in Backus Naur Form (BNF)
[
24
]
, PCFG
[
1
;
38
]
and attribute grammars
[
2
]
. Likewise, the search strategy in
Udrescu and Tegmark 2020
restricting to certain combinations of subterms on detected invariance can be understood as
CFG
. To incrementally improve predictions and knowledge
CFG
have been used for interactive feedback
[
29
;
7
]
.
Beyond grammars, random graph models
[
11
]
have bin used to learn the inherent structure of equations on
Wikipedia
.

4

Figure 2
:

We represent an expression (
2(a)
) in variables
x,y,z
with a tree
t\in T_{\Sigma}
(
2(b)
) and
c\in\Sigma^{(0)}
marking the positions of parameters
\theta_{c}=(7,12)^{T}
.
To evaluate a regular tree prior
p(t)
we construct a factor graph (
2(c)
):
For every node in
t
, there are
two
random variables,
x_{i}
and
q_{i}
.
The random variables
x_{i}
model the possible ranked symbols for a tree node and are thus observed, e.g.
x_{5}
corresponds to
\hbox to9.08pt{\vbox to9.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 6.92 L 0 6.75 C 0 10.57 3.1 13.66 6.92 13.66 L 5.65 13.66 C 9.47 13.66 12.57 10.57 12.57 6.75 L 12.57 6.92 C 12.57 3.1 9.47 0 5.65 0 L 6.92 0 C 3.1 0 0 3.1 0 6.92 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.38 6.92 L 1.38 6.75 C 1.38 9.8 3.86 12.28 6.92 12.28 L 5.65 12.28 C 8.71 12.28 11.18 9.8 11.18 6.75 L 11.18 6.92 C 11.18 3.86 8.71 1.38 5.65 1.38 L 6.92 1.38 C 3.86 1.38 1.38 3.86 1.38 6.92 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{3.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\in\Sigma^{(0)}
in
t
.
and assignments to the random variable
q_{5}
model the possible states a
PTA
can be in after parsing the tree
t
up to that symbol. Finally, factors
\phi_{j}
express the transition probabilities of the
PTA
.
To determine
p(t)
we condition on all
x_{i}
(i.e. the symbols in the input tree
t
) and marginalize over all possible state assignments to
q_{i}
(i.e. runs of the
PTA
).

We propose to model the solution space (
Section

4.1
) for symbolic regression in terms of tree languages together with probability distributions over that space. With that, the task of symbolic regression can be naturally expressed as a Bayesian inference step (
Section

4.2
),
and a multitude of prior knowledge (compare
Section

6.1
) can be expressed through probabilistic regular tree expressions.

4.1
Symbolic Expressions as Tree Languages

First, we divide symbolic expressions into their syntactic structure and parameters.

Definition 4.1

(Symbolic Expression)
.

Let
\Sigma
be ranked alphabet with special symbols
c,d\in\Sigma^{(0)}
. A symbolic expression
\mathfrak{e}=(t,\theta_{d},\theta_{c})\in\mathfrak{E}
is a tuple of a symbolic tree structure
t\in T_{\Sigma}
, discrete
\theta_{d}\in\Theta_{d}(t)
, and continuous
\theta_{c}\in\Theta_{c}(t)
parameter vectors, s.t.
\Theta_{c}(t):=\mathbb{R}^{|\operatorname{pos}_{c}(t)|}
and

\Theta_{d}(t):=\mathbb{Z}^{|\operatorname{pos}_{d}(t)|}
hold
with
\operatorname{pos}_{x}=\{d\in D|t(d)=x\}\;\forall x\in\Sigma
denoting the set of all positions of a symbol.

In other words,
the syntax is modeled through a tree language
and parameters are kept in separate vector spaces
\Theta_{d},\Theta_{c}
with special symbols
c,d\in\Sigma^{(0)}
marking their locations in the expression.

\Theta_{d},\Theta_{c}
can not directly be part of the ranked alphabet which is required to be
finite
.

Example 2
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma^{(0)}\cup\Sigma^{(1)}\cup\Sigma^{(2)}
with

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}),\hbox to11.56pt{\vbox to12.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 6.92 L 0 10.99 C 0 14.81 3.1 17.91 6.92 17.91 L 9.07 17.91 C 12.89 17.91 15.99 14.81 15.99 10.99 L 15.99 6.92 C 15.99 3.1 12.89 0 9.07 0 L 6.92 0 C 3.1 0 0 3.1 0 6.92 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.38 6.92 L 1.38 10.99 C 1.38 14.05 3.86 16.53 6.92 16.53 L 9.07 16.53 C 12.13 16.53 14.61 14.05 14.61 10.99 L 14.61 6.92 C 14.61 3.86 12.13 1.38 9.07 1.38 L 6.92 1.38 C 3.86 1.38 1.38 3.86 1.38 6.92 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{3.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
,
\operatorname{pos}_{c}=\{01,000\}\subseteq D
,
\operatorname{pos}_{d}=\{1\}\subseteq D
,
\Theta_{c}(t):=\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\Theta_{d}(t):=\mathbb{Z}
and e.g.
\theta_{c}=(0.25,1.06)^{T}\in\Theta_{c}(t)
,
\theta_{d}=2\in\mathbb{Z}
. This yields the symbolic expression
(\sin(0.25x)+1.06)^{2}
.

The unrestricted solution space of a symbolic regression task is

\mathfrak{E}:=\bigcup_{t\in T_{\Sigma}}\Big\{(t,\theta_{d},\theta_{c})\;\Big|\;\;\theta_{d}\in\Theta_{d}(t);\theta_{c}\in\Theta_{c}(t)\Big\}.

(3)

We assign probabilities
0\leq p(\mathfrak{e})\leq 1
to expressions
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{E}
such that overall

p(\mathfrak{E})=\sum_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{E}}p(\mathfrak{e})=\sum_{t\in T_{\Sigma}}\Bigg(\sum_{\theta_{d}\in\Theta_{d}(t)}\Big(\int_{\Theta_{c}(t)}p(t)p(\theta_{c}|t)p(\theta_{d}|t)\;d\theta_{c}\Big)\Bigg)=1

(4)

holds.
This construction, inspired from
[
8
]
for probabilistic programs, splits the measure into a measure
p(t)
for trees, as well as measures
p(\theta_{c}|t)
,
p(\theta_{d}|t)
for parameters.
While the latter can be realized through standard Lebesgue and counting measures,
p(t)=\frac{1}{Z}\tilde{p}(t)
is a
formal tree series
with
Z=\sum_{t\in T_{\Sigma}}\tilde{p}(t)<\infty
inducing a probability for tree structures.

4.2
Sampling-Based Bayesian Inference

Having defined symbolic expressions
\mathfrak{e}
in terms of tree languages, the task of symbolic regression given data
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
, is expressed through a Bayesian inference step as

\displaystyle p(\mathfrak{e}|\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})

\displaystyle\propto p(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}|\mathfrak{e})p(\mathfrak{e})\propto p(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}|t,\theta_{c},\theta_{d})\tilde{p}(t)p(\theta_{d}|t)p(\theta_{c}|t),

(5)

where we assume the data to be noisy observations
\mathcal{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(\operatorname{eval}_{\mathfrak{e}}(\mathcal{X}),\sigma)
of the evaluation of
\mathfrak{e}
at
\mathcal{X}
corrupted by Gaussian noise of some standard deviation
\sigma\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda)
,
whose value is jointly inferred.
Thus the likelihood
p(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}|t,\theta_{c},\theta_{d})
is Gaussian.
Finally, a tree series
\tilde{p}(t)
and prior distributions for the parameters
p(\theta_{d}|t)
,
p(\theta_{c}|t)
jointly express scientists’ prior knowledge.
We call the combination of these three priors a
regular tree prior

p(\mathfrak{e})
and show its usefulness for scientific symbolic regression.

(a)

Training Data

(b)

Prior

(c)

Posterior

(d)

Results

Figure 3
:
Particle system: system knowledge defines a prior distribution of potential force functions (
3(b)
), which results after inference with training data (
3(a)
) in a posterior distribution (
3(c)
). More data and combinations of knowledge (
3(d)
) help to find the true gravitational force. In (
3(a)
,
3(b)
,
3(c)
) colored paths show the true trajectories of the particles in the corresponding datasets.

The posterior
p(\mathfrak{e}|\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is approximated through sampling expressions
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}
(given
\mathfrak{e}
) from a Markov Chain with a
Metropolis-Hastings

[
25
;
12
]
transition kernel

\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{e}\rightarrow\mathfrak{e}^{\prime})=\mathcal{T}^{Q}(\mathfrak{e}\rightarrow\mathfrak{e}^{\prime})\mathcal{A}(\mathfrak{e}\rightarrow\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}),

(6)

composed of a proposal
\mathcal{T}^{Q}(\mathfrak{e}\rightarrow\mathfrak{e}^{\prime})
followed by a Hastings step with acceptance probability

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\mathfrak{e}\rightarrow\mathfrak{e}^{\prime})=\min\left[1,\frac{p(X,Y|t^{\prime},\theta_{c}^{\prime},\theta_{d}^{\prime})\tilde{p}(t^{\prime})p(\theta_{c}^{\prime}|t)p(\theta_{d}^{\prime}|t)}{p(X,Y|t,\theta_{c},\theta_{d})\tilde{p}(t)p(\theta_{c}|t)p(\theta_{d}|t)}\cdot\frac{p(u^{\prime})\mathcal{T}^{Q}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathfrak{e})}{p(u)\mathcal{T}^{Q}(\mathfrak{e}\rightarrow\mathfrak{e}^{\prime})}\det\Big(\frac{\partial(\theta^{\prime},u^{\prime})}{\partial(\theta,u)}\Big)\right],

(7)

that can be determined by evaluations of the tree series
\tilde{p}
.
Changing the tree structure
t
to
t^{\prime}
may affect the number of parameters contained in the corresponding expressions
\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}
. To make these jumps
reversible

[
10
]
(i.e.
\mathcal{T}^{Q}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathfrak{e})
exists) we sample as many auxiliary variables
u,u^{\prime}
to match the dimensions
\dim([\theta,u])=\dim([\theta^{\prime},u^{\prime}])
.
Jumps in that joint space
[\theta,u]
may change the volume corrected by a change of variable formula (
Section

A.1
).

4.3
Sampling Symbolic Expressions

Proposals should explore the space of potential symbolic expressions well but also exploit the experts’ prior knowledge of promising expressions.
To that end,
\mathcal{T}^{Q}(\mathfrak{e}\rightarrow\mathfrak{e}^{\prime})
splits into transit kernels for the tree structures
\mathcal{T}^{Q}(t\rightarrow t^{\prime})
and for the parameters
\mathcal{T}^{Q}(\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime})
.
While
\mathcal{T}^{Q}(\theta_{c}\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}_{c})
can exploit gradients and thus leverage efficient Hamiltonian Monte Carlo samplers,

\mathcal{T}^{Q}(t\rightarrow t^{\prime})
proposes discrete changes without gradient information.
These discrete proposals could be

(i)

global moves
by sampling (a variant of) the prior,

(ii)

local moves
by re-sampling contexts in the grammar (
Section

A.3
), or

(iii)

a neural network’s prediction.

We limit our experiments to the first option and leave other options as a promising direction for better exploration of structures in future work.
Once a new tree structure is determined, all occurrences

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
are replaced by samples
c\in\mathbb{R},d\in\mathbb{Q}
from the priors
p(\theta_{c}|t),p(\theta_{d}|t)
or a kernel
\mathcal{T}^{Q}(\theta_{c}\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}_{c})
respectively.

4.4
Regular Tree Prior Density Evaluation

To evaluate
p(\mathfrak{e})
and respectively
\tilde{p}(t)
, e.g., for the acceptance probability (
Equation

7
) of a proposal, we construct a
factor graph

[
18
]
that resembles the structure of the tree
t
to be evaluated as shown in
Figure

2
.
Recall that a run of a
PTA
is an assignment of states given a tree
t
as an input.
Each node in
t
corresponds to a pair of
random variables

q_{i},x_{i}
in the factor graph. The latent variable
q_{i}
models the state assignment in a run of the
PTA
(derived from
\tilde{p}
; compare
Section

2.2.1
) and the observed variable
x_{i}
models the input of a
ranked symbol
in position
i\in D\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{*}
, i.e.
t(i)
.
A factor
\phi_{j}\in V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{n+1}
in the graph is a tensor of rank
n+1
with
V_{k}=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{|Q|}\;\;\forall k
and corresponds to an entry of the transition function
\delta
of the
PTA
for a symbol of rank
n
, as illustrated in the following example.

Example 3

(Factor Graph)
.

Consider a
PTA
has read the symbol

t
in

x_{1}

q_{1}
models the state of the
PTA
before doing so.

\phi_{1}
assigns a probability for the

to start in a state assignment to
q_{1}
, reading
x_{1}
to be again

, and
transition
to individual state assignments for both,
q_{3}

. The factor
\phi_{0}

\mu
.

Since
\tilde{p}(t)
is the expectation of runs being successful, it corresponds to
p(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\sum_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}}\prod_{l}\phi_{l}
marginalizing all possible state assignments.

) yields unphysical predictions on the same data.

5
Prior Knowledge from Boolean Combinations

Science rarely occurs in a leap.
A hypothesis is built, rejected and refined many times.
Thus symbolic regression’s capabilities to incorporate prior knowledge must reflect this.
Key to the proposed formalism is its
closure under boolean operations
, which directly transfers from the known closure
[
30
]
of regular tree languages:
To find the law of
gravitational force
from observations of particle trajectories, one can exploit two assumptions
[
38
]
.
First, with
translational invariance
one can restrict the solution space to functions wherein positions of particles
p_{i},p_{j}
only occur as differences.
Second, with
dimensional analysis
, only quantities with matching units are allowed to be added or subtracted.

Xu et al. 2021
hand-craft a context-free grammar that conjoins these two aspects.
We represent the same knowledge with the intersection of two independently specified
pRTEs
(
Appendix

D
).

5.1
Orbiting Particle Systems

We apply this knowledge to an
N
-body system with a stationary particle of high mass at the center and
N-1
particles of random but lower mass orbiting around it.
We follow the setup of
[
38
]
but leave the particle masses
m_{i},m_{j}
observable.
Noisy training data
\{(p_{i},p_{j},m_{i},m_{j},v_{i},v_{j},f_{ij}+\epsilon)\}_{ij}
from the interaction of pairs of particles is derived from the system in
Figure

3(a)
.
We use three hold-out particle systems for testing.
The error of the learned force functions
f(p_{i},p_{j},m_{i},m_{j},v_{i},v_{j})
is shown in
Figure

3(d)
to decrease for an increasing amount of training data.
For the prior only encoding
translational invariance
the resulting function space is too broad to let the inference converge to a good solution within the computational budget.
Through combination with
dimensional analysis
, the true force law is recovered (
Figure

3(c)
) even though the prior (
Figure

3(b)
) was quite uninformative.
The remaining uncertainty stems from jointly estimating the gravitation constant.

But context-free grammars are not closed under Boolean operations
[
15
]
, prohibiting this incremental refinement of priors in general. With closure properties experts could express: I know it is either this (
E_{1}
) or less likely this (
E_{2}
) but not this (
E_{3}
); i.e.
(E_{1}\cup E_{2})\setminus E_{3}
.

\mathbf{8.41\;{\scriptstyle\pm 0.31}}

test3

1459.91

Existing benchmark datasets
[
22
;
34
]
often compromise a broad variety of equations
for which it is infeasible to come up with general knowledge for fitting functions.
Many scientific applications differ by

(i)

the existence of sophisticated but domain-specific knowledge about which solutions can be considered,

(ii)

the data sparsity due to expensive acquisition in experiments.

In this section, we show the scientific discovery process with case studies from soil and material science.

Experimental Setup

We randomly sample inputs and parameters for models from domain-specific literature (see
Section

B.1
) and corrupt the outputs with noise to generate challenging symbolic regression problems.
In order not to give an unfair advantage to our method, we have limited the usefulness of prior knowledge to such prior knowledge as is typically available for scientific problems, such as the structure of equations.
Most importantly, for the parameters
\theta_{c},\theta_{d}
in the expressions, we have chosen uninformative priors.
The true values for the parameters are on average
6.8466
standard deviations away from the mean of the prior distribution.
The ratio of p-values between the prior expectation and actual value looked up in the prior density is
0.0318
.
We compare the performance of our method with a variety of standard symbolic regression from genetic programming
[
19
]
, over sparse identification
[
3
]
to recent deep learning approaches
[
21
;
14
]
(details in
Appendix

A
).

6.1
Sorption Isotherms

The transition of ions or molecules from a solution to a solid phase is called
sorption
.
In literature, there exists a multitude of sorption isotherm equations to relate the
equilibrium sorptive concentration

c
(
\frac{\text{mg}}{L}
) in the solution phase and the
sorbate concentration

s
(
\frac{\text{mg}}{\text{Kg}}
) in the soil to characterize the retention of contaminants in soils and sediments
[
33
]
.
These models have been unified
[
13
]
as

s=s_{T}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\left(\frac{q_{ij}c^{\alpha_{ij}}}{1+p_{ij}c^{\beta_{ij}}}\right)^{\gamma_{ij}},

(8)

with each choice for
n,m_{0},\ldots,m_{n}
and assignment to parameters
s_{T},f_{i},q_{ij},p_{ij},\alpha_{ij},\beta_{ij},\gamma_{ij}
being a model that relates
s
and
c
.
All of these models share some regular pattern which we express with a regular tree expression
E_{\text{iso}}
as

Datasets.

We test generalization given a training dataset
\mathcal{D}_{\text{train}}
of
20
noisy observations on
10
different isotherms with datasets

(i)

from the same range as the training data
\mathcal{D}_{\text{test1}}\subseteq[20,100]
,

(ii)

lower concentrations
\mathcal{D}_{\text{test2}}\subseteq[0,20]
, and

(iii)

higher concentrations
\mathcal{D}_{\text{test3}}\subseteq[100,150]
.

Results

in
Table

1
show the
Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE)
statistics (mean
\pm
std) taken over
50
repetitions of the experiment for each combination of algorithm and dataset.
Here prior knowledge helps to generalize better in the majority (
17
out of
30
) of the test set scenarios.
Especially in extrapolation, i.e.
\mathcal{D}_{\text{test2}}
(9 out of 10), and
\mathcal{D}_{\text{test3}}
(5 out of 10), our method performs better.

To better understand these results, we analyzed the complexity in terms of the shortest description lengths
[
35
]
of the expressions found (see
Appendix

E
for full details):
Even though the encoded domain knowledge is quite general (compare
Figure

4
4(a)
) it helps
pRTE
to meet the right level of complexity of expressions (i.e. relative complexity
\approx 1
).
In contrast,
SInDy
and
GVAE
tend to generate too simple expressions and
Genetic Programming
generates overly complex expressions
causing
unphysical
behavior (compare
Figure

4
4(c)
).

PRTE
yields an informed posterior (
Figure

4
4(b)
) with estimates for uncertainties.

6.2
Hyper-Elastic Materials

Materials like rubber exhibit a non-linear behavior when stresses and strains are applied.
A common model class for
hyper-elastic materials
are
Ogden Models

[
27
]
, that relate the
strain energy density

W(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})=\sum_{p=1}^{N}\frac{\mu_{p}}{\alpha_{p}}\left(\lambda_{1}^{\alpha_{p}}+\lambda_{2}^{\alpha_{p}}+\lambda_{3}^{\alpha_{p}}-3\right)

(10)

to the eigenvalues of a deformation gradient tensor
C
, the
principal stretches

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
with parameters
\alpha_{p},\mu_{p}
.
We express this knowledge as an
pRTE

E_{\text{hyp}}
with shared parameters
\alpha
, as

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\end{aligned}\right)

(11)

with
\rho_{s}
denoting the probability of continuation.

Datasets.

We test generalization given a dataset
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}\in\mathcal{D}_{\text{train}}\subseteq[2.0,3.0]
of
20
observations with test sets:

(i)

\mathcal{D}_{\text{test1}}\subseteq[2.0,3.0]
with i.i.d. samples from the same range as the training data,

(ii)

\mathcal{D}_{\text{test2}}\subseteq[0.75,1.75]
with smaller stretches, and

(iii)

\mathcal{D}_{\text{test3}}\subseteq[3.25,4.25]
with bigger stretches.

Results

Table

2

summarizes the generalization on three synthetic Ogden materials.
Even though the expressed knowledge is weak (i.e. little constraints compared to arbitrary polynomials) it helped to better generalize in all of our experiments.
More complex material models which also can be expressed through
pRTE
are discussed in the appendix. The advantage of our method is that it can be adapted to this kind of knowledge as well, while standard symbolic regression approaches can not.

6.3
Limitations

We see two major limitations of our work:

(i)

If
knowledge is too broad
, too many equations are possible and the structure selection becomes a random walk with bad mixing. Bayesian reasoning with uniform-like, uninformative priors performs rather poorly in benchmarks
[
22
]
.

(ii)

If
knowledge can not be expressed
with regular expression; e.g. tree languages with equivalences of subtrees
[
30
]
or function space properties like the derivative in a certain point, our method can not be applied.

7
Conclusion

We showed that the use of tree languages comes with several advantages to encoding scientific prior knowledge as a prior distribution for symbolic regressions:

(i)

compact notation with regular tree expressions,

(ii)

closedness under boolean operations

.
Our scientific case study showed that reasoning with existing but general prior knowledge helped to better generalize sorption processes in soil science
17
out of
30
test set scenarios. When applied to the identification of hyper-elastic materials our method consistently outperformed all considered baselines.

Appendix A
Implementation Details

A.1
Determinant of differentiable jumps between parameter spaces

The constructions of the differentiable maps
f
to model jumps between parameter spaces of different sizes are taken from
Jin et al. 2019
.
However, the actual computation of the determinants of the jacobians that is needed for an implementation of their method is skipped by the authors.
Thus we provide this missing derivation for general jumps, considering both
expansion
(see
Section

A.1.1
) and
shrinkage
(see
Section

A.1.2
) of parameter vectors
\theta
.

A.1.1
Expansion of Parameters

Consider
f:(\theta,u)\mapsto(\theta^{*},u^{*})
with
u=\begin{pmatrix}u_{\theta}\\
u_{n}\end{pmatrix}
mapping to
\theta^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\theta+u_{\theta}}{2}\\
u_{n}\end{pmatrix}
and
u^{*}=\frac{\theta-u_{\theta}}{2}
that increases the number of parameters in a symbolic expression.
If the parameter vector expands from size
n
to size
n^{*}
one can determine the determinant of the Jacobian as follows:

\displaystyle\det\Big(\frac{\partial(\theta^{*},u^{*})}{\partial(\theta,u)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\theta^{*}}{\partial\theta}&\frac{\partial\theta^{*}}{\partial u}\\
\frac{\partial u^{*}}{\partial\theta}&\frac{\partial u^{*}}{\partial u}\end{pmatrix}

(12)

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{2}}^{(n+n^{*})}\cdot\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c | c}\begin{matrix}1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&1\\
0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}1&&&&\\
&\ddots&&&\\
&&1&&\\
&&&2&\\
&&&&\ddots\\
&&&&&2\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&1\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}-1&&&0&\cdots&0\\
&\ddots&&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&-1&0&\cdots&0\end{matrix}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{2}}^{(n+n^{*})}\cdot 2^{(n^{*}-n)}\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c | c}\begin{matrix}1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&1\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&1\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&1\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}-1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&-1\end{matrix}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{2}}^{(n+n^{*})}\cdot 2^{(n^{*}-n)}\det\Big(I_{n}\Big)\cdot\det\Big(I_{n}-I_{n}I_{n}^{-1}\cdot(-1)\cdot I_{n}\Big)

(17)

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{2}}^{(n+n^{*})}\cdot 2^{(n^{*}-n)}\cdot 2^{n}=2^{-n}

(18)

A.1.2
Shrinkage of Parameters

Consider
f:(\theta,u)\mapsto(\theta^{*},u^{*})
with
\theta=\begin{pmatrix}\theta^{0}\\
\theta^{d}\end{pmatrix}
mapping to
\theta^{*}=\theta^{0}+u
and
u^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}\theta^{0}-u\\
\theta^{d}\end{pmatrix}
that reduces the number of parameters in a symbolic expression.
If the parameter vector shrinks from size
n
to size
n^{*}
one can determine the determinant of the Jacobian as follows:

\displaystyle\det\Big(\frac{\partial(\theta^{*},u^{*})}{\partial(\theta,u)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\theta^{*}}{\partial\theta}&\frac{\partial\theta^{*}}{\partial u}\\
\frac{\partial u^{*}}{\partial\theta}&\frac{\partial u^{*}}{\partial u}\end{pmatrix}

(19)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c | c}\begin{matrix}1&&&0&\cdots&0\\
&\ddots&&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&1&0&\cdots&0\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&1\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}1&&\\
&\\
&&\ddots&&\\
&\\
&&&&1\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}-1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&-1\\
0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0\end{matrix}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{c | c}\begin{matrix}1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&1\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&1\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&1\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}-1&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&-1\end{matrix}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\Big(I_{n^{*}}\Big)\cdot\det\Big(I_{n^{*}}-I_{n^{*}}I_{n^{*}}^{-1}\cdot(-1)\cdot I_{n^{*}}\Big)

(24)

\displaystyle=2^{n^{*}}

(25)

A.2
Hyperparameters

The posterior distributions of symbolic solutions are approximated through
5000
samples taken after
15000
burn-in steps.
The samples are thinned by a factor of
100
to
50
effective samples.
Furthermore, we consistently chose
\lambda=1
, and
\rho_{s}=\rho_{p}=0.1
for all experiments.

The priors for the continuous parameters in the pRTEs are chosen
\mathcal{N}(1,0.1)
.
Here, the expectation of one avoids meaningless exponents and divisions by zero in too many proposals.
Only the prior for
S_{T}
is chosen
\text{Exp}(0.15)
to scale the expressions a-priori.
With these choices, the prior is not informative. Compare the function scatters in
Figure

4(a)
.

A.3
Context Re-Sampling

Incremental steps to an existing equation allow us to make better proposals and achieve better local connectivity of the Markov chain.
To that end, the partial evaluation of densities allows us to propose
local changes
to a tree structure
t
at hand, e.g. by only replacing a subtree of an already promising equation.
To achieve this, one needs to understand the evaluation of a tree series as the full marginal inference in the factor graphs. Not marginalizing a certain state variable
q_{r}
yields a distribution over states
\mu_{r}\in\Delta(Q)
for the tree position
r\in D
. This distribution describes the possible states for a
PTA
, given a partial tree
t\in
as
context
. Again, we use a special symbol
t(r)=\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to12.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 6.92 L 0 10.99 C 0 14.81 3.1 17.91 6.92 17.91 L 7.92 17.91 C 11.74 17.91 14.84 14.81 14.84 10.99 L 14.84 6.92 C 14.84 3.1 11.74 0 7.92 0 L 6.92 0 C 3.1 0 0 3.1 0 6.92 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.38 6.92 L 1.38 10.99 C 1.38 14.05 3.86 16.53 6.92 16.53 L 7.92 16.53 C 10.97 16.53 13.45 14.05 13.45 10.99 L 13.45 6.92 C 13.45 3.86 10.97 1.38 7.92 1.38 L 6.92 1.38 C 3.86 1.38 1.38 3.86 1.38 6.92 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{3.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\in\Sigma_{0}
to mark the position of the context state query, as shown in
Figure

5
.
We relax the categorical distribution
\mu_{r}
to a Boltzmann distribution
p_{B}(q_{r}=i)\propto\exp{\frac{\log\mu_{r}(i)}{\tau}}\;\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
with a temperature
\tau
that controls the exploration of a-priori unlikely states.
Sampling
q_{r}\sim p_{B}
and regrowing the tree
t
from position
r
(compare
Section

4.3
) yields a new proposal tree structure
t^{\prime}
.
This is similar to the local steps in
Jin et al. 2019
) but proposals are consistent with the prior knowledge by excluding states with zero probability in
\mu_{r}
.

:
Marginal inference for
p(q_{4})
in a factor graph (
5(b)
) to re-sample the context
t_{\leq r}
of a tree
t
in position
r\in D
marked by
?\in\Sigma^{(0)}
. With the inferred state
q_{4}
a new sub-tree can be grown.

A.4
Baselines

For all of our experiments, we used the following baseline implementations.

A.4.1
Genetic Programming (GP)

Here, we use the implementation of
genetic.SymbolicRegressor
provided by
gplearn
package with
250
generations for the genetic evolution. All other hyper-parameters are left at the default values.

A.4.2
Sparse Identification (SInDy)

Here, we solve an ordinary
LASSO
regression problem (using the implementation in
Sci-kit Learn
with
10^{7}
iterations) by interpreting the feature matrix
\Phi(\mathbf{X})
as a
library of terms
and enforced sparsity on the solution

\displaystyle\hat{\xi}=\arg\min_{\xi}\;\;(\mathbf{Y}-\Phi(\mathbf{X})\xi)^{T}(\mathbf{Y}-\Phi(\mathbf{X})\xi)+\alpha\|\xi\|_{1}

(26)

with a hyper-parameter
\alpha=0.1
. This allows for a sparse identification of the actual symbolic expression in a postprocessing step. Let
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
be
m
data samples with dimension
n
each. Then
\mathbf{X}^{P_{i}}
denotes the matrix with all polynomial terms of degree
i
for each of the
m
samples. The library

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{X})=\begin{pmatrix}|&|&|&|&|&|&|&|&|\\
1&\mathbf{X}&\mathbf{X}^{P_{i}}&\sin(\mathbf{X})&\cos(\mathbf{X})&\tan(\mathbf{X})&\tanh(\mathbf{X})&\frac{1}{X}&\sqrt{(X)}&\ldots\\
|&|&|&|&|&|&|&|&|\end{pmatrix}

(27)

is composed from stacked matrices
\mathbf{X}
in combination with functions of choice. We include polynomials up to a degree of
i\leq 6
and also
\log(X+\epsilon_{1})

\exp(X/\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2})
with paramaters
\epsilon
to avoid exploding or ill-defined values.

A.4.3
Grammar Variational Autoencoder (GVAE)

We implemented a variant of the autoencoder that can deal with continuous variables inside of a symbolic expression. For the training, we put a special symbol in the grammar which marks the position of parameters and trained on
10^{5}
random equations with a batch size of
2048
until convergence after roughly
20
epochs. At inference time, to compute the acquisition function of the latent GP, we first decode a latent code
z\in\mathbb{R}^{16}
to a symbolic structure and use
100
steps of a
BFGS
optimizer with learning rate
10^{-2}
to solve for the continuous parameters first. All other parameters are left at the default values from
Kusner et al. 2017
.

A.4.4
Deep Generative Symbolic Regression (DGSR)

Here, we used the implementation released by
Holt et al. 2023
(
https://github.com/samholt/DeepGenerativeSymbolicRegression
) and used the provided pre-trained checkpoints of the transformer model for our inference. For the sorption experiments, the checkpoint
"1-covar-koza"
for a single independent variable is used. For the hyper-elastic material experiments we respectively use the checkpoint
"3-covar-koza"
. All other hyperparameters and configs are left at their default values.

Appendix B
Experimental Setup

B.1
The Synthetic Sorption Isotherm Dataset

As described in
Section

6.2
, we chose standard isotherm equations from literature to generate challenging symbolic regression tasks for the scientific domain of soil science. The ground truth equation is only used to generate the data and is presented to the regression algorithms at no time. After choosing an equation from the literature, we sample values for the parameters that occur inside it from the following priors:

\displaystyle s_{T}

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda=0.015)

\displaystyle k,k_{1},k_{3},f_{1},f_{2},\alpha

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda=4)

\displaystyle k_{2}

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda=100)

\displaystyle X,X_{c}

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda=0.03)

\displaystyle K_{F}

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda=0.05)

For
Redlich Peterson
and
Toth
we chose:

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda=0.75)

and for
Brunauer Emmett Teller
we chose:

\displaystyle k_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda=0.25)

\displaystyle k_{2}

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda=4)

\displaystyle k_{3}

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda=100)

and for
Two Site Langmuir
we chose:

\displaystyle k_{1},k_{2}

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda=8).

Afterward, the inputs
c
to the equations are uniformly sampled according to the intervals in
\mathcal{D}_{\text{train}},\mathcal{D}_{\text{test1}},\mathcal{D}_{\text{test2}},\mathcal{D}_{\text{test3}}
and the outputs
s
are corrupted with white noise
\epsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu=0,\sigma=0.1)
. These input/output pairs
(c_{i},s_{i}+\epsilon_{i})
form the dataset for our symbolic regression task.
Furthermore, we fix the random seeds of the dataset generation (with an individual seed for each dataset and parameter sampling) to make the setup reproducible over repetitions and comparison against baselines.
The implementation of the data generation process is included in the code accompanying this submission.

B.2
Hyper-Elastic Material Models

For the experiment, we choose a ground truth
Ogden Hyper-Elastic Material Model
with
N=2
,
\alpha=(1.75,2.5)^{T}
, and
\mu=(1.5,0.1)^{T}
. The input principal stretches
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
to this equation are uniformly sampled according to the intervals in
\mathcal{D}_{\text{train}},\mathcal{D}_{\text{test1}},\mathcal{D}_{\text{test2}},\mathcal{D}_{\text{test3}}
and the outputs
w
are corrupted with white noise
\epsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu=0,\sigma=0.01)
. These input/output pairs
(\lambda_{1i},\lambda_{2i},\lambda_{3i},w_{i}+\epsilon_{i})
form the dataset for our symbolic regression task.

Appendix C
Extended Examples

C.1
Theory Examples

This section provides additional (and longer) examples for the definition of trees, symbolic expressions and probability distributions over it.
First,
Example

4
details how a tree is sampled from an
pRTE
.

g
symbol. Let’s craft a regular expression (
E_{1}
) that equivalently expresses this knowledge:

We start with a variable
y\in V
which is not part of the alphabet
y\notin\Sigma
but a special symbol. All intermediate steps of a generation are trees
t\in T_{\Sigma\cup V}
and the generation ends when
t\in T_{\Sigma}
. First,
\infty y
says that all
y
must be iteratively replaced with the inner expression

replaces all occurrences of

Let’s turn it into a probabilistic regular tree expression (
E_{2}
) by making the choice operator
+
probabilistic. The weights in front of each element determine the probability of by chosen:

The purpose of the following
Example

5
is to show how a tree structure (e.g. sampled following the procedure from the previous example) is enriched with tuples of parameters to form a symbolic expression that can be evaluated.

,
\operatorname{pos}_{c}=\{01,0000\}\subseteq D
,
\operatorname{pos}_{d}=\{1\}\subseteq D
,
\Theta_{c}(t):=\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\Theta_{d}(t):=\mathbb{Q}
and e.g.
\theta_{c}=(0.25,1.06)^{T}\in\Theta_{c}(t)
,
\theta_{d}=2\in\mathbb{Q}
. This yields the symbolic expression
(\sin(0.25x)+1.06)^{2}
.

C.2
More Hyper-Elastic Material Models

In literature exist several hyper-elastic material models which can be expressed as prior knowledge with our proposed method as
pRTE
for Bayesian inference. These, non-exclusively compromise
Neo Hookean Solids
(
Section

C.2.1
),
Mooney Rivlin Solids
(
Section

C.2.2
), and
Generalized Rivlin Solids
(
Section

C.2.3
).

C.2.1
Neo Hookean Solid

This is a particular case for the
Ogden Material Model
as presented in the main paper (see
Equation

11
) with
N=1,\alpha_{1}=2,\mu_{1}=C_{1}\alpha_{1}
which yields

W=C_{1}\cdot(I_{1}-3)=C_{1}\cdot(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{3}^{2}-3)

(28)

expressed as

(29)

with a (real) parameter for the occurrence of symbol
C_{1}
.

C.2.2
Mooney Rivlin Solid

Another common model from literature is the
Mooney Rivlin Solid

W=C_{1}\cdot(\bar{I}_{1}-3)+C_{2}\cdot(\bar{I}_{2}-3)

(30)

with

\displaystyle\bar{I}_{1}

\displaystyle:=J^{-\frac{2}{3}}\cdot I_{1}

\displaystyle\bar{I}_{2}

\displaystyle:=J^{-\frac{4}{3}}\cdot I_{2}

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle:=\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle:=\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}\lambda_{3}^{2}+\lambda_{3}^{2}\lambda_{1}^{2}

expressed as

with (real) parameters associated with the occurrence of symbols
C_{1}
,
C_{2}

C.2.3
Generalized Rivlin Model

The previous model can be generalized to

W=\sum_{p,q=0}^{N}C_{pq}(\bar{I}_{1}-3)^{p}\cdot(\bar{I}_{2}-3)^{q}+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{D_{m}}(J-1)^{2m}

(32)

expressed as

\displaystyle E_{\text{GRM}}

Appendix D
Incremental Combination of Knowledge for a Newtonian Physics Priors

In our experiments, we considered the combination of different priors that reflect different knowledge an expert can have about the forces present in a
particle system
derived from Newtonian physics. Each assumption can be independently expressed through an
pRTE
as visualized in
Table

3
. The last column shows the combination of knowledge which can be automatically derived using boolean operations. The first row shows the different priors over force functions when used to integrate the dynamics of a test particle system. The second row is a short description of the knowledge that is contained in the prior, followed by a corresponding
pRTE
. Note that we reduced the search space to be able to visualize the resulting
PTA
in the next row. All experiments in the main paper do
not
use this version but the full search space and knowledge as described in
Xu et al. 2021
. For the visualization of the state machines we follow the convention from
Weidner 2015
and depict states as nodes, and an arrow between nodes iff the transition probability after reading a symbol
s\in\Sigma^{(1)}
is greater than zero. For symbols
s\in\Sigma^{(2)}
of rank two, there are two consecutive states and thus the solid arrow depicts the transition for the left child, while the dashed arrow depicts the transition for the right child. Outgoing arrows depict the final states. Recall that the automaton accepts when there is a run that transitions to only final states in the leaf nodes.

Appendix E
Complexity Analysis

We conducted a
complexity analysis
of the expressions found by the different algorithms to better understand the generalization performance of
pRTE
.
As a measure of the complexity of expressions, we used the idea of
description length
from information theory.
The more bits are required to describe a model, the more complex it is considered.
The same approach is followed by
Udrescu et al. 2020
and results in the combined description length for both parameters and structure of an equation as detailed in
Table

4
.
Symbolic expressions are considered parameterized functions for which each node within its tree structure is considered a basis function.
For instance, the expression
y\cdot(2\cdot x+7)
has a description length of
\mathcal{L}_{D}=5\cdot\log_{2}(4)+\mathcal{L}_{D}([2,7])
resulting from the
5
occurrences of the
4
unique
basis functions
\{\cdot,+,x,y\}
.

Table 4
:
Defintion of
description length
for symbolic expressions following
Udrescu et al. 2020
.

Object

Symbol

Description length
\mathcal{L}_{D}

Natural Number

n

\log_{2}(n)

Integer

m

\log_{2}(1+|m|)

Rational Number

\frac{m}{n}

\mathcal{L}_{D}(m)+\mathcal{L}_{D}(n)

Real number

r

\log_{+}(\frac{r}{\epsilon}),\;\;\;\log_{+}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}(1+x^{2})

Parameter vector

\mathbf{p}

\sum_{i}\mathcal{L}_{D}(p_{i})

Parameterized function

f(x;\mathbf{p})

\mathcal{L}_{D}(\mathbf{p})+k\log_{2}n;\;\;\text{$n$ basis functions appeared $k$ times}

The results are summarized in
Figure

6
.
The scatters show the performance of several equations found by the different algorithms in terms of
relative complexity

\frac{\hat{\mathcal{L}_{D}}}{\mathcal{L}_{D}}
and
prediction error (RMSE)
on an out-of-distribution dataset (
D_{\text{test2}}
).
Here,
\hat{\mathcal{L}_{D}}
is the description length of the symbolic expression found by the algorithm and
\mathcal{L}_{D}
is the description length of the ground truth model the data was generated from.
The vertical lines indicate the mean relative complexity of expression found by a particular algorithm while the highlighted areas are a standard deviation to it.

Throughout all different setups
pRTE
is closest to a relative complexity of
1
.
This means that the expert knowledge in the tree priors helps to better find the right level of complexity of expression for the problem which we hypothesize leads to
pRTE
’s superior prediction performance
in out-of-distribution datasets.
While the implicit priors encoded to
GVAE, SInDy
lead to too simple expressions, the expressions found by genetic programming are often close in complexity to a small neural network (muli-layer perceptron with
12
hidden units and
\tanh
activations).

Figure 6
:
Complexity analysis of the found symbolic expressions for different problems and algorithms:
solid dots indicate the expected relative complexity compared to the average prediction performance (RMSE) on the out-of-distribution test set
\mathcal{D}_{test2}
.
Shaded areas visualize the corresponding standard deviations. The ground truth model (vertical black line) has a relative complexity of
1.0
.

pRTE
yields solutions that are closest to this ideal complexity and thus generalize better.
